## Reading the form decision run

Hand-written, outside the generated block. Written from the plan trees, same rule as above.

### What was compared, and what was deliberately not

The generated block above measures **three arms × 16 probes**. The three synthetic shapes —
point read by id, `projectId`-filtered listing, listing with no selective predicate — carry
**no tenant predicate of their own**, which is the whole design of that half: with the query
silent about the tenant, the policy is the only thing restricting rows, so the difference
between arms IS the policy's cost. The other 13 probes are the application's actual cases,
entered as the SQL mirrors whose fidelity `--phase` runs prove on every capture. Reading a
shape as if it were an application query is the one mistake this section exists to prevent,
and §S2 below is exactly where it would be made.

Two guards make the arms comparable rather than merely fast. Every arm's rows are compared
against every other arm's for the same probe, **as whole rows and across every sweep**, and
the run refuses to write anything if they disagree — a policy that lets different rows
through is answering a different question, and a policy that lets NOTHING through is
trivially the fastest. And the swap is transaction-scoped: each arm installs its policy on
all three trio tables, reaches `omnipost_app` with `SET LOCAL ROLE` in the same transaction,
measures, and rolls back, so the restore is the rollback itself rather than a repair step
that could fail. The three shipped policies were re-read afterwards and are byte-identical on
all five attributes (printed in the block above).

### The verdict, and the adjudications it owes

**`W` wins, by the pre-declared tiebreak.** Not by a hair, and not by a number: **not one of
the 16 probes clears BOTH conditions** a difference must clear to be the form's. This run
separates the two conditions cleanly, which the previous one did not, and that makes it the
better illustration of why the rule needs both halves. `S3` moved **outside** the band
(20 µs, 1.11 %) but its `S − W` sign FLIPPED across the five sweeps — −32, +12, +8, +33,
+2 µs — so it measures the run, not the form. `S2` did the converse: its sign is **stable**
across all five sweeps, every one favouring `S`, but the whole move is 4 µs, **inside** the
band on the absolute limit. Out-of-band-but-unstable and stable-but-in-band are both
unattributable, and the two candidate forms are therefore indistinguishable by this harness —
which is what a pre-declared tiebreak is FOR.

`S2` is also the clearest demonstration of why the band is an OR of an absolute and a
relative limit rather than either alone. At −7.14 % a relative-only band would call it out of
band and hand `S` an attributable win on 4 µs of a 56 µs probe; at 4 µs the absolute limit
correctly holds it inside. The band's shape, declared before this run, is what keeps a
sign-stable microsecond from deciding the policy form of 61 tables.

That conclusion is the opposite of what a single sweep says, which is the finding worth
keeping. Six consecutive single sweeps of this same comparison announced `S` wins, `W` wins,
`UNDECIDED`, `UNDECIDED`, `S` wins and `UNDECIDED` — `Q4` alone ranged from 111 µs in `S`'s
favour to 112 µs in `W`'s. Pooled over 15 samples that same probe settles at **+2 µs /
0.12 %**, inside the band. A decision rule reading one sweep would have picked a policy form
for 61 tables out of the resolution of its own instrument.

**The `A′` → `W` comparison is a different question and is adjudicated here**, because the
band applies to it too and an artifact reporting only the favourable cases does not satisfy
the requirement:

| Probe      | `A′`  | `W`   | Δ                 | Adjudication                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| ---------- | ----- | ----- | ----------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `S3`       | 4.163 | 1.804 | **2.31× faster**  | **Accepted — this is the change's purpose.** The unselective shape is where the per-row `current_setting()` call is the whole plan. Inside task 4.6's expected 2.1–2.4× scan-node band.                                                                                                                                                  |
| `Q3`       | 4.209 | 1.866 | **2.26× faster**  | **Accepted — same mechanism**, on the real account-wide feed. Inside the same band.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| `Q4`       | 3.808 | 1.723 | **2.21× faster**  | **Accepted — same mechanism.** Inside the same band.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| `Q1`       | 0.046 | 0.034 | 1.35× faster      | **Accepted.** 12 µs on a 20-row page; the qual is evaluated over fewer candidate rows, so the win is proportionally smaller.                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| `Q2`       | 0.042 | 0.029 | 1.45× faster      | **Accepted.** 13 µs, same mechanism as `Q1`.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| `Q5`       | 0.044 | 0.033 | 1.33× faster      | **Accepted.** 11 µs, same mechanism.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| `S2`       | 0.016 | 0.056 | **3.50× SLOWER**  | **Accepted, bounded, and named — the one regression.** See §The wrapped form moves two plans below.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| `S1`       | 0.006 | 0.006 | no measurable Δ   | **Accepted.** The previous capture read 2 µs slower here and explained it as two InitPlans amortising over a single row; at this resolution that cost is not separable from zero. Both readings are inside the band, and the honest statement is that the point read is where the form stops mattering — not that it costs exactly 2 µs. |
| `Q6`–`Q13` | —     | —     | ≤ 2 µs either way | **Inside the band.** The child reads and point lookups touch too few rows for the qual's form to matter; `Q6` is the exception and moves its plan — below.                                                                                                                                                                               |

### The wrapped form moves two plans — recorded, not omitted

A moved plan is a finding even when the rows are identical, and this run produced two. Both
are visible in the generated tables' node and index columns.

**`S2` — the regression.** Under `A′` the planner chooses `Post_projectId_createdAt_idx` and
a plain `Index Scan`, whose output is already ordered, so no `Sort` node is needed. Under
`W` and `S` alike it switches to `Post_projectId_archivedAt_idx` with a `Bitmap Heap Scan`
plus an explicit `Sort`, and the scan-node median goes 0.016 → 0.056 ms. The 15 samples do
not overlap (`A′` 0.014–0.025, `W` 0.048–0.072 — both ranges are printed in §S2 of the block
above), so this is a real effect and not the noise that governs the W-vs-S question. It also
reproduces independently in the retired four-arm capture below (0.024 → 0.063), taken before
anyone was looking for it. The plausible mechanism — stated as plausible, because nothing
here measures it — is that a qual reading InitPlan `Param`s is estimated differently from one
calling `current_setting()` per row, and the changed estimate changes the index choice.

Why it is accepted rather than repaired: **`S2` is a synthetic shape, not a query this
application issues.** It exists to strip the tenant predicate out so the policy stands alone.
Its real counterpart is `Q1`/`Q5` — the same `projectId`-filtered listing as the repository
actually emits, carrying its own `accountId` and `projectId` predicates — and those keep
`Post_accountId_projectId_idx` under all three arms and get **faster**, 0.046 → 0.034 and
0.044 → 0.033. The regression is confined to the shape that removed the predicate the
application always supplies. It is recorded because a plan that moves under a form change is
exactly the class of surprise the 58-table sweep has to look for (its own per-policy evidence
pass carries this same obligation), not because anything here is broken.

**`Q6` — the same mechanism, the other direction.** `Hash Join → Index Scan → Hash → Seq
Scan` under `A′` becomes `Nested Loop → Index Scan → Index Only Scan` under both wrapped
forms, newly using `Project_id_accountId_key`, and the case gets slightly faster (0.084 →
0.082, inside the band). Recorded for symmetry: the wrapper moves plans in both directions,
so "the plan moved" is not by itself a regression signal.

**The sweep's own evidence pass inherits more than this obligation.** Task 8.9 rewrites 57
policies plus the `AIPromptTemplate` variant, and its per-policy digest pass binds ONE tenant
— so it is blind in exactly the way task 2.5 closed here for the trio: under a bound tenant, a
rewritten policy that lost its `__system__` disjunct returns identical rows and ships green,
having silently revoked every `withSystemContext()` flow's visibility. 8.9's evidence pass
MUST therefore carry the three-GUC-state check — bound tenant, `__system__`, UNSET — per
policy, not only the row digest.

### `A′+init` was renamed `W`; `B′` and `B′+sys` were retired

The four-arm comparison recorded below is **history, kept deliberately**. Its `A′+init` arm
and today's `W` are the SAME form — the shipped policy with each `current_setting()` wrapped
in `(SELECT ...)` — so its numbers remain readable under the new name. `B′` and `B′+sys` are
no longer measured: `B′` carries no `__system__` escape and therefore could never ship (every
`withSystemContext()` flow would see nothing under it), and `B′+sys`, the only shippable
member of that family, measured indistinguishable from the shipped form. Keeping them in the
arm list would cost a full transaction per sweep and invite a reader to compare against a
form nobody can commit. The measurements below are what was actually observed and are not
retracted.

### The retired four-arm results

Execution medians of three runs (ms), from the retired comparison:

| Shape                                    | `A′` (shipped) | `A′+init` (= today's `W`) | `B′`  | `B′+sys` |
| ---------------------------------------- | -------------- | ------------------------- | ----- | -------- |
| `S1` point read by id                    | **0.014**      | **0.014**                 | 0.109 | 0.114    |
| `S2` `projectId`-filtered listing        | **0.024**      | 0.063                     | 0.120 | 0.128    |
| `S3` listing with no selective predicate | 5.403          | **3.009**                 | 3.774 | 5.577    |

Note that `S2`'s regression under the wrapped form is present in this older capture too
(0.024 → 0.063), measured before anyone was looking for it.

### `B′` never becomes an index probe either — and that is the load-bearing correction

The research's expectation for `B′` was "one `InitPlan`, evaluated once, followed by an index
probe on `Post(projectId)`, which already exists". Half of that is confirmed and half is not:

```text
Index Scan  rel=Post  idx=Post_projectId_createdAt_idx
            Index Cond = ("projectId" = 'tif-ab-a-proj-0001')     <-- from the QUERY
            Filter     = (hashed SubPlan 1)                        <-- the policy
  Seq Scan  rel=Project  rows=100  (SubPlan 1, loops=1)
```

The subplan IS evaluated once (`Actual Loops: 1`, ~0.08 ms to build the hash). But the index
condition comes from the QUERY's own `projectId` predicate, not from the policy — the policy
is a hashed membership `Filter` on top. On `S3`, which supplies no `projectId`, `B′` still
sequential-scans `Post`. So `B′` does not convert a scan into a probe; it changes what the
per-row filter costs.

### What actually drives the per-row cost: the `current_setting()` call, not the column form

`S3` is where this becomes unambiguous, because all four arms use the same
`Limit → Sort → Seq Scan` and differ only in the filter expression. Comparing the sequential
scan node alone:

| Arm       | Filter on the `Post` scan                                   | Scan node — embedded plan (ms) | Scan node — independent re-capture (ms) | Total `S3` median (ms) |
| --------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------ | --------------------------------------- | ---------------------- |
| `A′`      | two `current_setting()` calls per row                       | 3.881                          | 4.146                                   | 5.403                  |
| `A′+init` | two InitPlan constants (`$0`, `$1`) per row                 | **1.833**                      | **1.775**                               | **3.009**              |
| `B′`      | one hashed set-membership per row                           | 2.517                          | 2.498                                   | 3.774                  |
| `B′+sys`  | one `current_setting()` call **plus** the hashed membership | 4.22                           | 4.205                                   | 5.577                  |

**Read the two scan-node columns as single-run readings — the two readings that predate the
scan-node median.** When this table was first published the harness computed a median for the
STATEMENT but not for an individual plan node, so each scan-node figure comes from one run:
the first column is that capture's last-run plan, and the second is the same node in an
independent re-capture by the re-gate. The harness NOW computes a per-node median across runs
(advisory 1.2 of `tenant-rls-cost-repair`), and the regenerated `--policy-ab` block above
carries those medians pooled over 15 samples (`A′` 4.163 → `W` 1.804 ms on the scan node;
5.438 → 2.993 ms on statement time — `A′+init` is today's `W`, same form, new name); this
hand table is kept as the historical two-reading record. The
`Total S3` column is the 3-run statement median the script computed even then. An earlier
version of this table quoted a single figure per arm that showed `A′` and `B′+sys` as exactly
equal; that equality was a one-run coincidence and is not reproducible, so the two columns
are published instead of it.

`B′+sys` is still what settles the question, and the finding survives being stated precisely.
It is `B′` with the `__system__` arm added back, and **its scan node is indistinguishable from
`A′`'s**: across captures the two sit between 3.88 and 4.22 ms, a spread (0.34 ms) comparable to the
drift between captures of the SAME arm (`A′`'s own is 0.27 ms). `B′`'s entire advantage is erased by adding that one
disjunct back. The disjunct that mentions no column is the expense; the set-membership half is
nearly free once its hash exists.

What is **not** inside that spread is `A′+init` at ~1.8 ms — **~2.1-2.4× cheaper than either** across
the four pairings, which is the gap the follow-up is about. On the current capture's 15-sample
medians the same gap reads **2.31× on the scan node** and **1.82× on statement time** — the
~2.1-2.4× band is a SCAN-NODE band, so a statement-time ratio below it is the statistic
differing, not the gap closing. So the recorded win for `B′` on `S3`
is not a win for the set-membership FORM at all: it is a win for not calling
`current_setting()` per row, and `A′+init` collects that same win while keeping the shipped
policy's semantics, its column, and its escape.

### Recording it plainly, including where B′ wins

- **`A′` wins the two selective shapes outright**: `S1` 0.014 vs 0.109 (×7.8) and `S2` 0.024
  vs 0.120 (×5.0). On both, `B′` pays a full `Seq Scan` of `Project` to build its hash for a
  query that reads one or twenty rows. That cost is fixed per statement and scales with
  **projects per account**, exactly as the research predicted it would.
- **`B′` wins the unselective shape** — `S3` 3.774 vs `A′`'s 5.403 — and that is recorded
  plainly rather than explained away. It is also the only shape it wins, it wins it for a
  reason that is not its own form, and the version of it that could actually ship (`B′+sys`,
  5.577) **loses** the same shape.
- **`A′+init` beats every other arm on `S3`** (3.009) and ties `A′` on `S1`.
- **`A′+init` costs something on `S2`** (0.063 vs 0.024), and this is the one result that
  argues against it. The plan flipped index: `Post_projectId_archivedAt_idx` plus a `Sort`
  instead of `Post_projectId_createdAt_idx` walked in order. Wrapping the GUC read changes
  the estimated cost of the qual, and that moved a cost comparison the planner was making
  narrowly. A policy rewrite can therefore move plans in BOTH directions, and any follow-up
  that adopts it has to re-run these 13 cases rather than assume the `S3` win generalizes.

### The revisit trigger, verbatim, and its state

> if B′ measures well AND Slice 2 classifies many more than three tenant-owned tables → the
> per-table choice reopens (A′ and B′ are not mutually exclusive per table).

**First half: NOT met.** B′ measures well on exactly one of three shapes, and only in the
form that cannot ship. Its shippable form is at best a tie and at worst a loss on all three.
The second half is not yet knowable — the keyless-model triage has not run — but it does not
matter while the first half is unmet, and the trigger is a conjunction.

**And the guarantee is not on the table either way.** B′ and B′+sys are row-VISIBILITY rules.
Neither prevents a `Post` row from carrying a tenant key that disagrees with its project's;
they only stop such a row from being returned. The composite foreign key makes that row
unrepresentable, holds against a `BYPASSRLS` role, and holds when the GUC is unset. No policy
form provides that at any speed, which is why a B′ that had won all three shapes would still
not have been an argument for replacing A′ — only for pairing with it on tables where the
column is genuinely not worth carrying.

## Index shortlist run — four arms over `Post`

Captured 2026-09-10T02:32:54.567Z in 3.4 s. PostgreSQL: PostgreSQL 16.14 (Debian 16.14-1.pgdg12+1). Each arm installs ONE index shape inside a single transaction, reaches `omnipost_app` with `SET LOCAL ROLE` in that same transaction, binds `app.account_id` to `tif-ab-a`, measures every probe, and ROLLS BACK. Nothing is committed: the rollback IS the restore, so there is no repair step that could itself fail, and `Post`'s index inventory is re-read afterwards and compared as `(indexname, indexdef)` tuples.

**This is a SHORTLIST FILTER, not the authoritative capture.** Every number below was taken against an index that exists only inside an open transaction. The winner is committed by its own migration and re-measured against the COMMITTED index before any figure here is quoted as the shipped one.

**Re-run this exact comparison:**

```bash
node --import tsx --conditions development --env-file=.env \
  scripts/rls-ab-measurement.ts --index-ab --projects 100 --posts 10000 --runs 3 --repetitions 5
node --import tsx --conditions development --env-file=.env \
  scripts/rls-ab-measurement.ts --cleanup
pnpm exec prettier --write docs/reports/TENANT_RLS_AB_MEASUREMENT.md
```

### The arms

| Arm   | Shape                | Key                                       | Note                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| ----- | -------------------- | ----------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `IX0` | as-shipped (control) | `("accountId", "projectId")`              | the live index, untouched — the control this comparison is measured against                                                                                                                                                                               |
| `IX1` | +createdAt extension | `("accountId", "projectId", "createdAt")` | extends the shipped key with the column every listing orders by, so a plan that takes it can also take its ordering — for a `projectId`-bound scan                                                                                                        |
| `IX2` | feed shape           | `("accountId", "createdAt")`              | drops `projectId` from the key so `createdAt` LEADS under an accountId-only bind — the account-wide feed's ordering, which the extension cannot supply                                                                                                    |
| `IX3` | DROP                 | _(no accountId-led index)_                | no accountId-led index at all. A first-class arm: if no candidate measurably beats the control, an index nothing selects is write-path and storage cost with no read behind it, and dropping is the measured answer rather than a failure of the exercise |

Every candidate is partial `WHERE "deletedAt" IS NULL`, because the shipped index is — an arm that dropped the predicate would be measuring a different index as well as a different key. Candidate names follow Prisma's own `Table_col_col_idx` convention, so each arm installs the exact object its migration would install. `CONCURRENTLY` is never used: it cannot run inside a transaction, which is the containment these arms depend on.

### The preconditions that make an in-transaction index believable

An index built inside an open transaction still yielding index-only scans WITHIN that transaction is an inference, not a citation. These three make it evidence instead:

1. **`VACUUM (ANALYZE)` runs BEFORE the arm transaction** — it cannot run inside one — so the visibility map is settled before any arm opens.
2. **No DML on the measured table inside the arm.** Any write clears visibility-map bits for the pages it touches and silently destroys index-only eligibility. The arms issue DDL, `ANALYZE`, `SET LOCAL ROLE`, `set_config`, the probe statements and their `EXPLAIN`s — nothing else.
3. **`Heap Fetches: 0` is ASSERTED on every `Index Only Scan` node**, and a non-zero or ABSENT counter aborts the arm rather than being reported as an index-only result.

`ANALYZE "Post"` runs in-transaction in EVERY arm, the control included, so the planner sees statistics refreshed the same way on all four rather than the control alone planning against different bookkeeping.

**What the assertion actually inspected**, so its scope is a measured fact rather than a claim — an assertion with no subjects is a gate that cannot fail, and this run says outright how many it had. The last column is the one that matters for the inference: an index-only scan on a PRE-EXISTING index proves that index-only scans work, which nobody doubted; only one on an index built INSIDE the arm transaction says anything about whether such an index is usable there.

| Arm   | Index-only-scan nodes inspected | On                                 | Of those, on the index THIS ARM BUILT |
| ----- | ------------------------------- | ---------------------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| `IX0` | 15                              | `Project.Project_id_accountId_key` | _(builds none)_                       |
| `IX1` | 15                              | `Project.Project_id_accountId_key` | 0                                     |
| `IX2` | 15                              | `Project.Project_id_accountId_key` | 0                                     |
| `IX3` | 15                              | `Project.Project_id_accountId_key` | _(builds none)_                       |

The assertion inspected **60** index-only-scan node(s) across the arms and every one of them fetched zero heap pages, so no arm was aborted as unrepresentative.

**The inference is NOT fully converted, and the honest statement is the narrow one.** Every index-only scan above is on an index that ALREADY EXISTED when the arm opened; **not one plan took an index-only scan on an index the arm itself built**. So what this run demonstrates is that the visibility map is settled and index-only scans are clean inside the arm transaction — which is the precondition the `VACUUM` and the no-DML rule exist to establish, and it is worth having. What it does NOT demonstrate is the narrower claim that an index built inside an open transaction is itself usable index-only within it: the planner never chose one of those indexes for an index-only scan on this corpus, so the assertion had no chance to observe it. That bounds this mode's validity rather than invalidating it — the arms' figures are ordinary index and sequential scans, which are not subject to the visibility-map question at all — and the winner is re-measured against the COMMITTED index anyway, where the question does not arise. It is recorded because an unconverted inference quietly reported as converted is exactly the defect this assertion was added to prevent.

### Restore proof

`Post`'s index inventory, read from `pg_indexes` before the first arm and again after the last one, compared as `(indexname, indexdef)` TUPLES — the definition travels with the name because an arm re-creating an index under the same name with a different key would leave a name-only proof reporting an identical inventory:

```text
Post_accountId_projectId_idx :: CREATE INDEX "Post_accountId_projectId_idx" ON public."Post" USING btree ("accountId", "projectId") WHERE ("deletedAt" IS NULL)
Post_deletedAt_idx :: CREATE INDEX "Post_deletedAt_idx" ON public."Post" USING btree ("deletedAt")
Post_id_accountId_key :: CREATE UNIQUE INDEX "Post_id_accountId_key" ON public."Post" USING btree (id, "accountId")
Post_pkey :: CREATE UNIQUE INDEX "Post_pkey" ON public."Post" USING btree (id)
Post_projectId_archivedAt_idx :: CREATE INDEX "Post_projectId_archivedAt_idx" ON public."Post" USING btree ("projectId", "archivedAt") WHERE ("deletedAt" IS NULL)
Post_projectId_createdAt_idx :: CREATE INDEX "Post_projectId_createdAt_idx" ON public."Post" USING btree ("projectId", "createdAt") WHERE ("deletedAt" IS NULL)
Post_projectId_publishedAt_idx :: CREATE INDEX "Post_projectId_publishedAt_idx" ON public."Post" USING btree ("projectId", "publishedAt") WHERE ("deletedAt" IS NULL)
Post_projectId_scheduledAt_status_idx :: CREATE INDEX "Post_projectId_scheduledAt_status_idx" ON public."Post" USING btree ("projectId", "scheduledAt", status) WHERE ("deletedAt" IS NULL)
Post_projectId_status_idx :: CREATE INDEX "Post_projectId_status_idx" ON public."Post" USING btree ("projectId", status) WHERE ("deletedAt" IS NULL)
Post_scheduledAt_idx :: CREATE INDEX "Post_scheduledAt_idx" ON public."Post" USING btree ("scheduledAt") WHERE ("deletedAt" IS NULL)
```

The two reads are identical, which is what makes the swaps provably transaction-scoped.

### The decision rule, declared before the run

**Computed from the medians below, not typed in.** The rule is applied in code (`decideIndex`), so this section cannot say one thing while its own table says another.

- **The band**: a move is INSIDE it when it is ≤ 6 µs **or** < 1 % of its own baseline — the same band the form run uses, and an OR for the same reason: an absolute-only band calls every sub-millisecond probe out-of-band for a few microseconds of wobble, a relative-only band calls a 7 µs probe a 100 % regression.
- **The statistic**: the SCAN-NODE median over the POOLED 15 sample(s) — 3 EXPLAIN run(s) × 5 independent sweep(s) — per probe, on that probe's own measured relation.
- **The direction**: `IX0` (as-shipped) is the baseline; each candidate is the candidate.
- **Attributability**: a difference counts as the SHAPE's only when it is out of band AND every sweep agreed on its sign. Measured necessity, not caution — six consecutive sweeps of the FORM comparison on this same corpus put one probe anywhere from 111 µs in one arm's favour to 112 µs in the other's. **A single sweep is never sign-stable**: with one sweep there is one sign, so `every sweep agreed` is vacuously true and the test stops testing while still printing `sign-stable` beside a verdict. At `--repetitions 5` this run can attribute a difference; below two it could not.
- **Admissibility**: an arm is admissible when it has ZERO attributable regressions on an APPLICATION case. A synthetic-shape regression is recorded and adjudicated but does not disqualify — the shapes deliberately omit the predicates the application always supplies, and the form run measured exactly that trap when `S2` regressed 3.5× under both candidate forms while its real counterparts `Q1` and `Q5` got faster.
- **Measurement first**: among admissible arms with at least one attributable case improvement, the most improvements wins; ties go to the fewest shape regressions.
- **The tiebreak**, and ONLY when no arm has an attributable case improvement: the pre-declared preference order **IX3 > IX0 > IX2 > IX1** decides among the admissible arms. It is a write-path order, which is the only question left when the reads cannot be told apart: every index is paid for on every `INSERT` and every `UPDATE` of its columns, so DROP is first; the as-shipped control is second because keeping it needs no migration at all; the two replacements come last, narrower before wider. The order is a tiebreak and never overrides a measured regression.

> **This rule was authored before the run that produced the numbers below, and the `tasks.md` entry for this work unit pre-declared no tiebreak of its own** — unlike the form run, whose tiebreak was written into task 2.7. Declaring one afterwards, with the medians already visible, would be choosing the rule that produces the preferred answer. It is recorded as a declared deviation in this change's apply progress.

### The verdict

**`IX1` — +createdAt extension, `("accountId", "projectId", "createdAt")`.**

`IX1` improves 2 application case(s) (Q1, Q5) by an amount attributable to the SHAPE — outside the band AND sign-stable across every sweep — with no attributable case regression, so the measurement decides and the pre-declared preference order does not apply.

| Arm   | Admissible | Case improvements | Case regressions | Shape improvements | Shape regressions |
| ----- | ---------- | ----------------- | ---------------- | ------------------ | ----------------- |
| `IX1` | yes        | Q1, Q5            | —                | —                  | —                 |
| `IX2` | **no**     | Q5                | Q2               | —                  | —                 |
| `IX3` | **no**     | Q5                | Q2               | —                  | —                 |

**The shipped index `Post_accountId_projectId_idx` was selected under the control arm by**: `Q1`, `Q2`, `Q5` — read from the plans, so the DROP arm's cost is a measured one rather than an assumed one.

### The index mechanics, stated rather than implied

With only `accountId` equality-bound — which is what the policy supplies when the query itself names no project — the scanned range of `(accountId, projectId, createdAt)` is ordered by `(projectId, createdAt)`, **not** by `createdAt`. So the `+createdAt` extension does NOT by itself give ordered output for the account-wide feed: Incremental Sort needs a leading sorted key, and B-tree skip scan — which could otherwise hop `projectId`'s distinct values — is a PostgreSQL 18 feature while this server is PostgreSQL 16.14 (Debian 16.14-1.pgdg12+1). `(accountId, createdAt)` is the shape that CAN order that feed, and it is in the arm list for exactly that reason rather than for symmetry.

### The single-corpus caveat

**The measurement rests on one shape** — 100 projects × 10 000 posts, exactly 100 per project — **which is the condition under which the displaced index wins.** A corpus with a different fan-out (a few enormous projects, or thousands of tiny ones) moves the selectivity of every `projectId`-led index in this comparison and can move the verdict with it. This caveat travels with the decision wherever the decision is recorded; it is not a disclaimer about precision, it is the boundary of what was measured.

## Reading the index shortlist run

Hand-written, outside the generated block. Written from the plan tables above, same rule as
the other reading sections.

### The verdict, and what decided it

**`IX1` — the `+createdAt` extension, `("accountId", "projectId", "createdAt")` partial
`WHERE "deletedAt" IS NULL`.** The measurement decided it and the pre-declared preference
order never came into play: `IX1` is the only arm that improves an application case by an
amount attributable to the shape AND regresses none.

The mechanism is visible in the plan column rather than inferred from the timing. Under the
as-shipped index, `Q1` and `Q5` run `Limit → Sort → Index Scan`: the shipped
`("accountId", "projectId")` key carries no ordering, so a `LIMIT 20` over
`ORDER BY "createdAt" DESC` has to sort first. Under `IX1` both become `Limit → Index Scan`
with **no Sort node at all** — the extension puts `createdAt` inside the range the query
already binds, so the index supplies the ordering the sort was manufacturing. That is a
structural change, not a faster sort: `Q1` 0.047 → 0.019 ms and `Q5` 0.048 → 0.019 ms, with
every one of the five sweeps agreeing on the sign.

**This is the displacement repair, measured.** The §Reading the after capture section records
`Q1 ×2.39` and `Q5 ×3.52` as regressions caused by the shipped index displacing the
`projectId`-led indexes that supplied ordering. The extension is what gives the ordering back
without giving up the tenant-led key.

### The two candidates that lost, and why they are not close calls

`IX2` and `IX3` are **inadmissible**, and by the same mechanism in both: with no
`("accountId", "projectId")` prefix available, `Q2`'s count falls from an `Index Scan` to
`Bitmap Heap Scan → Bitmap Index Scan` on `Post_projectId_archivedAt_idx` and pays 16-17 µs
for it, sign-stable across all five sweeps. An arm that regresses a real application case does
not win on the strength of what it improves elsewhere — the rule says so before the numbers,
and this is the case it was written for.

`IX3` (DROP) deserves its own sentence because the spec makes it a first-class outcome and
this run does **not** take it. The shipped index is not dead weight on this corpus: it is
selected by `Q1`, `Q2` and `Q5` under the control arm, read from the plans. Dropping it makes
`Q5` faster (0.048 → 0.015, the planner falls back to the ordered `Post_projectId_createdAt_idx`)
and `Q1` and `Q2` slower. DROP was measured, it was admissible as an arm, and it lost on
evidence rather than on preference.

### Every case that moved outside the band, with its adjudication

Nine case rows moved outside the ≤ 6 µs / < 1 % band. All nine are named here, including the
ones that are inconvenient for the winner:

| Probe | Arm   | Δ       | Attributable | Adjudication                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| ----- | ----- | ------- | ------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `Q1`  | `IX1` | −28 µs  | yes          | **Accepted — this is the repair.** The `Sort` node disappears; the listing reads the index in `createdAt` order.                                                                                                                                      |
| `Q5`  | `IX1` | −29 µs  | yes          | **Accepted — same mechanism**, on the command-side listing.                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| `Q5`  | `IX2` | −34 µs  | yes          | **Recorded, and it does not carry `IX2`.** Faster than the winner on this one case, by falling back to `Post_projectId_createdAt_idx`. `IX2` is inadmissible on `Q2` regardless.                                                                      |
| `Q5`  | `IX3` | −33 µs  | yes          | **Recorded**, same fallback, same disposition.                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| `Q2`  | `IX2` | +16 µs  | yes          | **Regression — disqualifies the arm.** `Index Scan` → `Bitmap Heap Scan`; the count loses its `(accountId, projectId)` prefix.                                                                                                                        |
| `Q2`  | `IX3` | +17 µs  | yes          | **Regression — disqualifies the arm**, identical mechanism.                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| `Q1`  | `IX2` | +19 µs  | no           | **Recorded as directional, not counted.** The sign flipped across sweeps, so the rule refuses to attribute it — but it points the same way as `Q2`'s attributable regression, and the arm is already out on that.                                     |
| `Q1`  | `IX3` | +19 µs  | no           | **Same**, same disposition.                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| `Q4`  | `IX2` | −235 µs | no           | **The largest absolute move in the run, and it is credited to nobody.** The sign flipped between sweeps. `Q4` is the same probe that ranged ±112 µs across single sweeps in the form run; it is unstable here for the same reason and the rule holds. |

No synthetic shape moved outside the band under any arm.

### What this corpus could NOT decide: the account-wide feed

The honest bound on this run, and it is a real one. `S3`, `Q3` and `Q4` — the unselective,
account-wide shapes — run `Seq Scan` under **every** arm, `IX2` included. The
`("accountId", "createdAt")` shape exists in the arm list precisely to serve that feed, and
the planner never took it.

That is not evidence that it cannot: **this corpus has two tenants**, so `accountId` selects
half the table, and no index beats a sequential scan at 50 % selectivity. A production corpus
with hundreds of tenants makes `accountId` selective, and the same shape could then win the
feed it lost here. So the feed question is **not decided by this run in either direction**,
and no later link may read `IX2`'s loss as a finding about `(accountId, createdAt)` on a
realistic tenant distribution. It is a finding about this corpus.

This compounds with the generated block's mechanics note rather than replacing it: even with
a selective `accountId`, the `IX1` extension cannot order the account-wide feed, because with
only `accountId` bound its range is ordered by `("projectId", "createdAt")`. The winner
repairs the project-scoped listings and leaves the feed exactly where it was, which is what
the numbers above show and all they show.

### What this section does not claim

- **It is a shortlist filter.** Every figure was taken against an index that existed only
  inside an open transaction. The winner is committed by its own migration and re-measured
  against the committed index; that later capture is the authoritative one, and if it
  disagrees with this section, it wins.
- **The `Heap Fetches: 0` assertion is only partly converted.** It inspected 60 index-only
  scans and every one was clean, but all 60 were on a pre-existing index — no plan took an
  index-only scan on an index an arm built. The generated block states this in full; it is
  repeated here so a reader of the prose alone does not inherit the stronger claim.
- **No write-path number.** Every measurement here is a read. A wider index costs more on
  every `INSERT` and every `UPDATE` of its columns, and this run measures none of that. The
  extension adds one column to an index that already exists, so the additional cost is small
  and bounded — but "small and bounded" is reasoning, not a measurement, and it is labelled
  as such.
- **Nothing about `Post_projectId_createdAt_idx`'s redundancy is settled here.** `Q5` reaching
  it under `IX2`/`IX3` shows it is live and useful, which is an input to that separate
  question, not an answer to it.

## Completion — Slice 1

Hand-written. The independence statement is a deliverable of this slice, not a summary of it.

### The composite foreign key is enforced independently of RLS configuration

`Post`, `PostContent` and `PostMedia` each carry a two-column foreign key to their parent's
`(id, accountId)` TOTAL unique. Re-measured from `pg_constraint` after this capture, on the
same database:

| Constraint                          | Child         | Parent    | `ON UPDATE`     | `ON DELETE`   | `MATCH`      | `convalidated` |
| ----------------------------------- | ------------- | --------- | --------------- | ------------- | ------------ | -------------- |
| `Post_projectId_accountId_fkey`     | `Post`        | `Project` | `a` (NO ACTION) | `c` (CASCADE) | `s` (SIMPLE) | **true**       |
| `PostContent_postId_accountId_fkey` | `PostContent` | `Post`    | `a` (NO ACTION) | `c` (CASCADE) | `s` (SIMPLE) | **true**       |
| `PostMedia_postId_accountId_fkey`   | `PostMedia`   | `Post`    | `a` (NO ACTION) | `c` (CASCADE) | `s` (SIMPLE) | **true**       |

Two consequences, stated separately because they are different claims:

1. **The guarantee is retroactive, not forward-only.** `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` ran and passed,
   and `convalidated = true` is the catalog saying so. Had it not run, the honest statement
   would have been that the constraint covers only rows written after it was added; that is
   not the situation, and this line exists so a future reader does not have to guess which
   one it was.
2. **It does not depend on row security at all.** It is referential integrity, checked by the
   engine on every write, so it holds for a role with `BYPASSRLS`, for a session with no
   `app.account_id` bound, for raw SQL, and for any future ORM. The `BYPASSRLS` arm of
   `apps/api/tests/integration/tenant-composite-fk.test.ts` asserts the connecting role
   really does bypass before drawing that conclusion, so the claim is measured rather than
   assumed.

The reverse is equally true and must not be blurred: **the FK's strength is not RLS being
fixed.** The FK cannot stop a correctly-parented row from being READ by the wrong tenant.
That is row security's job, and row security's status is per environment.

### RLS enforcement status, per environment

| Environment             | `relrowsecurity` on the trio | `FORCE` | Table owner | Application role audited                       | Status                                                                       |
| ----------------------- | ---------------------------- | ------- | ----------- | ---------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Development (this host) | **true** ×3                  | false   | `postgres`  | yes — `omnipost_app`, NOSUPERUSER, NOBYPASSRLS | **enforced** for application traffic; the owner channel is not subject to it |
| CI                      | true ×3 (same migrations)    | false   | `postgres`  | yes — the app-role channel runs the suites     | **enforced** for the suites that connect on the app channel                  |
| Production              | n/a                          | n/a     | n/a         | **not performed**                              | **RED** — there is no deployed environment, so nothing has been audited      |

`FORCE` is deliberately off (`docs/technical/ADR-0022-rls-enforcement-posture.md`): the owner
channel runs migrations and the seed, and forcing row security on the owner would break both.
The consequence is stated rather than left implicit — **any connection that arrives as the
table owner is not subject to these policies**, which is why the role split, not the policy,
is what makes RLS load-bearing.

An environment whose role audit was deferred stays red in this table. Production has never
been audited because it does not exist yet; the row says so instead of being omitted.

### What Slice 1 delivers, and what it does not

Delivered: the tenant column on all three trio tables, `NOT NULL`, backfilled and validated;
three composite foreign keys, validated over history; the two TOTAL uniques they target; RLS
enabled with one `tenant_isolation` policy per table; guard enrollment at 61 models with 1:1
policy parity; the write paths threaded from a guard-scoped parent resolution; the required
`TenantScope` on eight trio collection reads with a compile-time pin that now runs in CI; and
this before/after evidence with its regressions and their adjudications.

Not delivered, and named so the slice is not read as more than it is: the keyless-model
triage; the rest of the query contract; the three named residual collection reads
(`findByStatus`, `findReadyForPublishing`, `findWithFilters`) tracked as SMELL-92; the policy
rewrite and the index-shape decision this capture recommends; and any production posture at
all.

## Spike — Prisma shared-scalar relation pattern

### The question, and why the trio does not ask it

The Post trio dodges this by construction: `Post.accountId` participates in exactly one
relation (the composite one to `Project` — there is deliberately no direct `Account`
relation and no `Account.posts` back-relation), and likewise for the children toward
`Post`. The models that will hit it are the Slice 2 ones that ALREADY carry a direct
`Account` relation and would gain a composite parent relation over the same `accountId`
column. The pattern has to be decided before any of them migrate, which is what this spike
does.

### Protocol as run

Throwaway `spike.prisma` outside the repo, a throwaway `tif_spike` database on the same
server, dropped at the end. The model under test:

```prisma
model SpikeWidget {
  id        String @id @default(uuid())
  parentId  String
  accountId String
  label     String

  // Relation 1 — the direct Account navigation the Slice 2 models already carry.
  account SpikeAccount @relation(fields: [accountId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)

  // Relation 2 — the composite parent relation. `accountId` is shared with
  // relation 1; that sharing IS the spike.
  parent SpikeParent @relation(fields: [parentId, accountId], references: [id, accountId],
                                onDelete: Cascade, onUpdate: NoAction)

  @@index([accountId, parentId])
}
```

Prisma CLI 7.9.1, PostgreSQL 16.14. `prisma db push` was **not** used: its built-in
AI-consent guard refuses to run unattended, and that guard is right, so the DDL was taken
from `migrate diff --script` and applied to the throwaway database statement by statement —
byte-identical SQL, no destructive verb.

| #   | Protocol step                           | Command                                                             | Result                                                                                                                       |
| --- | --------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 1   | `prisma validate`                       | `prisma validate`                                                   | **PASS** — "The schema at spike.prisma is valid"; no warning about the shared scalar                                         |
| 2   | `prisma migrate diff`                   | `migrate diff --from-empty --to-schema spike.prisma --script`       | **PASS** — emits BOTH foreign keys; the composite one carries `ON UPDATE NO ACTION ON DELETE CASCADE`                        |
| 3   | Generated-client typecheck              | `tsc --strict --exactOptionalPropertyTypes` over five create shapes | **PASS** — flat, checked, nested-under-parent, nested-under-account and `createMany` all compile                             |
| 4   | Negative control for step 3             | same, over three deliberately wrong shapes                          | **RED demonstrated** — exit 2 with TS2353 / TS2741 / TS2741; the typecheck can fail                                          |
| 5   | `db pull` round-trip                    | `prisma db pull` then `diff`                                        | **PASS** — both relations survive verbatim, including the shared `accountId` in both `fields` lists and `onUpdate: NoAction` |
| 6   | Runtime behaviour (beyond the protocol) | generated client against the throwaway database                     | see below — two hazards the protocol does not ask about                                                                      |

The DDL Prisma generated for the two coexisting constraints, read back from `pg_constraint`:

```text
SpikeWidget_accountId_fkey           FOREIGN KEY ("accountId") REFERENCES "SpikeAccount"(id)
                                       ON UPDATE CASCADE ON DELETE CASCADE
SpikeWidget_parentId_accountId_fkey  FOREIGN KEY ("parentId", "accountId")
                                       REFERENCES "SpikeParent"(id, "accountId") ON DELETE CASCADE
```

### What the generated input types actually do with a shared scalar

This is the useful part, and it is better than expected. Nesting removes the shared scalar
from the input entirely rather than making it optional:

| Input type                                      | Shape                                           |
| ----------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------- |
| `SpikeWidgetUncheckedCreateInput`               | `{ id?, parentId, accountId, label }`           |
| `SpikeWidgetCreateInput` (checked)              | `{ id?, label, account, parent }`               |
| `SpikeWidgetUncheckedCreateWithoutParentInput`  | `{ id?, label }` — **no `accountId`**           |
| `SpikeWidgetUncheckedCreateWithoutAccountInput` | `{ id?, parentId, label }` — **no `accountId`** |
| `SpikeWidgetCreateManyParentInput`              | `{ id?, label }`                                |

So a nested create under the parent has no way to state a tenant key at all: Prisma derives
it from the parent row. That is exactly the derivation the threading design wants, and it is
enforced by the compiler rather than by review.

### Two hazards, measured rather than reasoned about

Both come from KEEPING relation 1 alongside relation 2. Neither is a toolchain failure, and
neither is visible in the three protocol steps.

1. **Two connects that disagree are accepted silently, and the PARENT wins.** A checked
   create with `account: { connect: { id: "acct-B" } }` and a parent belonging to `acct-A`
   type-checks, executes without error, and lands the row under **`acct-A`**. Prisma
   resolves the shared scalar from one relation and drops the other's value without a
   diagnostic. A caller who believed the `account` connect was the tenant assertion has
   written a row into a different tenant and been told it succeeded.
2. **`ON UPDATE CASCADE` on relation 1 rewrites the child's tenant key past relation 2's
   `NoAction`.** `UPDATE "SpikeAccount" SET id = 'acct-A2'` propagated into
   `SpikeWidget.accountId` and was accepted. The composite relation's `onUpdate: NoAction`
   exists precisely to keep a tenant re-point noisy (design D-S1-1); a sibling relation
   carrying Prisma's default `Cascade` over the same column defeats it.

The controls that make those two statements observations rather than guesses: a flat create
with a mismatched `(parentId, accountId)` pair IS refused (`P2003`), and a nested create
under the parent DOES land the parent's account (`acct-A`). So the engine is enforcing the
composite FK; hazard 1 is the input layer choosing a value before the FK ever sees a
conflict, and hazard 2 is a legal cascade the FK has no reason to refuse.

### Decision

**The protocol verdict is PASS: the shared-scalar pattern is viable on this toolchain.**
Nothing in steps 1-5 failed, so the design's named fallback rule ("any of the three fails →
fallback = the Post pattern") is NOT triggered, and this section does not pretend otherwise.

**The recommendation is nevertheless the Post pattern for Slice 2** — composite parent
relation only, `Account` navigation through the parent — on hazards 1 and 2 rather than on a
protocol failure. The Post pattern removes both by removing relation 1: with one relation
over `accountId` there is no second connect to disagree with, and no second `ON UPDATE` rule
to outvote `NoAction`. This is the pattern Slice 1's trio already uses, so it also keeps one
shape across both slices.

If a Slice 2 model is later argued into keeping its direct `Account` relation, these are the
guardrails the evidence says it needs, and they belong in that model's own decision record:
pin `onUpdate: NoAction` on **both** relations, and forbid the checked two-connect create
shape at the repository boundary so hazard 1 cannot be written.

**The rejected fallback stays rejected**, and this spike gives no reason to revisit it:
declaring the composite FK in raw SQL while the schema keeps a single-column relation would
have `prisma migrate dev`'s drift detection schedule the constraint's DROP in the next
generated migration. A guarantee the toolchain keeps trying to delete is not a guarantee.

### Re-running the spike

Nothing of it is committed: the schema, the throwaway database, the generated client and the
typecheck files were all created outside the repo and removed. The steps above are complete
enough to rebuild it — a `spike.prisma` with the model shown, a `prisma.config.ts` pointing
at a throwaway database, then `validate` → `migrate diff --script` → apply → `generate` →
`tsc` → `db pull` → `diff`.
